The Embassy has no knowledge of any authentic report
of plan for "more decisive steps" such as blockade of
Chinose ports if boycott continues. No "official spokes-

the 7th beyond telling of its receipt. The note proved
exceedingly difficult to translate into Japanese, because
the language has no relative pronouns and qualifying clauses
have to be throwm into adjective form. This caused some
confusion, but it as not important as the public has
state-
accepted the note with little or no excitement as a ./
ment of the American position. Yoshizawa, the ne Foreign
Minister, is to take office tonight. I am to call on him
tomorro.: when I am informed I shall receive the reply of the
Japanese Government both in Japanese and English. The

Count Yasuya Uchida was formally installed as
894.0
Minister of Foreign Affairs this morning. After in-
stallation he gave an oral interview to Japanese news-
paper men in which he stated that Japan's foreign policy
will iemain unchanged and that while Japan will claim
what is due, Japan dislikes to act unnecessarily against
the interests of other powers; that misunderstanding of
other powers will be dispelled in due course but that
"any blunder on Japan's part may lead to grave conse-
quences".

10th;
One. Japan believes that the racial self de-
termination of the inhabitants of Manchuria is necessary

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Tokyo, May 24, 1933.
No. 401.
Subject: Changes in Japanese Foreign Office Personnel.
F/HS
CONFIDENTIAL.
PN EC0
894.021/34
CopynitE
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NUH 12 1933
Department of State
JUN 2 3 1933
FILED
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
$ir:
As I reported briefly in my telegram No. 99
V May 16, 4 n.m., Mr. Hachiro Arita tendered his resignation
as Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs on May 15th. On
May 16th Mr. Mamoru Shigemitsu was appointed to fill his
place.
The resignation of Mr. Arita is of greater significance
than appears on the surface, if reports which have reached
this

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.95)
-2-
this Embassy can be credited. These reports have come
from several different sources and are all of the same
tenor, indicating that they are founded on fact. Ac-
cording to these reports the resignation of Mr. Arita
(allegedly due to ill-health) was in fact due to the
continued conflict between the chauvinistic and the
L.
moderate elements in the Foreign Office. Mr. Arita,
a trained diplomatist of the moderate school, has been
in conflict for some time past with the chauvinistic
f
element, represented principally by Shiratori, of the
Maoyhi
Bureau of Information and Inteljigence, and Tand, of
OOR
the Bureau of Asiatic Affairs. The ill-feeling between
Arita and the chauvinists reached a climax t the time
being a staunch advocate of remaining in the League,
while the chauvinistic element desired immediate with-
drawal. While Arita was instructing the J-panese
diolomatic representatives : broad to ssure the various
foreign offices that Japan has no immediate intention of
withdrawing, Shiratori was telling the press renresenta-
tives in Tokyo that Janan Woule withdraw in the near
future. A distinct rupture between Arit: on the one
hand and Shiratori and T-ni on the other took nl:ce
at this time, and as the chauvi istie lement had the
backing of the Foreign Minister himself nd of the military
authorities, Arita found it acviscble to rcsign. The
significance of the incident lies in the fact that the
chauvinistic

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
chauvinistic element in the Japanese Government maintains
strength enough to compel changes in the Foreign Office
personnel, and, further, in the fact that the Japanese
Government has lost another official representing the
more moderate elements.
Mr. Arita, who was friendly to the United States
V
and sincerely believed in the paramount importance
to Japan of close and friendly relations with the United
States, was the principal contact man for this Embassy
with the Foreign Office and could always be relied upon
to give a sympathetic hearing to any representations,
formal or informal, which the Embassy might make. His
resignation, therefore, is a distinct loss to the Embassy.
八
A Confidential Biographic Data Report on Mr. Mamoru
Shigemitsu, Mr. Arita's successor as Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs, is being forwarded in the same pouch
which carries this despatch. Mr. Shigemitsu is also a
trained diplomatist, having served in Germany, England,
the United States and China. He held the post of Japa-
nese Minister to China when the Shanghai Incident of
Jonuary, 1031, occurred, nd conducteu for Jaoan the
truce negotiations which closed that incident. In a
Japanese celebration 1 ter in Shanchai, he was injured
by a bomb thrown by a Korean m lcontent, and as a result
had one of his legs amputateu. For this reason he comm nds
the sympathy of the nation, nd his present appointment
is rumored to be due in part to this sympathy. His
appointment, however, is in lar er meesure due to the
fact

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
fact that he is a deep-thinking, level-headed, deliberate
and calm man, as well as a trained diplomatist. It is
reported that Viscount Uchida, the Minister for Foreign
Affairs, did not want an ardent militarist for Vice
Minister, as such an appointment would have forfeited the
confidence in the Foreign Office not only of nations abroad
but of a section of the Japanese public as well. He chose,
therefore, a trained diplomatist who was not too antagonistic
to the plans and methods of the military party. Mr. Shigemitsu
is not a militarist, but he is friendly with the leaders of
the Japanese Army today, many of whom are from Kyushu(as
is Mr. Shigemitsu), and because of his long residence in
China he is familiar with Japanese problems in China and
the Japanese Army's methds of liquidating these problems.
His appointment, therefore, is generally considered to
indicate  stronger Japanese policy tovard China on the
part of the Foreign Office, and in one newspaper(the HOCHI) is
hailed as the first step in the establishment of an "independent
diplomacy".
With the resignation of Arita, it annear d that the
Japanese Foreign Office would be more than ever under the
v √
influence of the ultra-nati nulist group, advocating an
"indenendent diplomacy", a strong hand in the Far East and
an Asiatic Monroe Doctrine. It was, therefore, with a great
deal of surorise that the diplomatic corps in Tokyo learned,
on May l8th, that olans were afoot to r move Shiratori and
Tani

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Tani from the Foreign Office and to send them to posts
in the field, Shiratori as Minister to Sweden and Tani
as Counsellor of the Japanese Embassy to "Manchukuo".
These appointments have not been gazetted or officially
announced as yet, but Foreign Office officials do not
deny that Shiratori is slated for the post at Stockholm,
if the Swedish Government consents. It does not appear
to be so certain that Tani will go to Changchun.
Various stories are told as to the reasons for the
removal of Shiratori as well as Arita from the personnel
of the Foreign Office. One(and the most reasonable) is
to the effect that, if Shiratori remained in office
without the braking effect of Arita, intense conflict
would occur between the old-line dinlomatic officers in
the field and the Foreign Office, with a probable deluge
of resignations from such men as Matsudaira, Debuchi,
Nagai and Saito. Another story is that for some time past
the officers in the field have been compl:ining because
Shiratori, with his blunt and ruthless methods of disclosing
the plans of the Japanese Government,has been injuring
their work and prestige abroad. The Foreign Minister therefore
felt it necessary to take some action and asked Shiratori to
accept a post in the field. Shiratori(vho has very strong
backing) refused to accept a field post unless Arita, who
he claimed was also in part responsible for the conflict in the
Foreign Office, was sent out to the field at the same time.
The Foreign Minister agreed and approached Arita with a
proposal

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
proposal to go to Turkey as Japanese Ambassador, but
Arita recognized the proposal as an attempt to remove him
from the Foreign Office and instead of accepting, re-
signed entirely from the service. The Foreign Minister,
however, having succeeded in reroving Arita, felt obliged
to fulfill the bargain and remove Shiratori as well.
Another account is to the effect that the Foreign Minister
simply is tired of the constant warfare in the Foreign
Office and has decided to oust the ring-leaders of both
factions.
It is unnecessary to indicate to the Department that
the removal of Shiratori from the active staff of the
Foreign Office will possibly mean en importont change in
the attitude of that department toward Western nations,
although it is doubtful if the basic policies of the
Government will be altered. Shiratori is credited in
Janan with the honor of being the leader, among the
civilian authorities, of the ultra-nationalistic movement.
The late Mr. Koku Mori, the leader in the Diet of the
"Back to Acia" movement, is renorted to have been simnly
a sookesman for Shiratori, who admitted on one occasion
that he wrote Mr. Mori's "Back to Asia" speech. Shiratori,
Meri and certain militury officers are renorted to have
composed the clicue which initiated Japan's "independent"
or "positive" diplomccy, which sun"orted Janan's military
activities in China, brought about the recognition of
"Manchukuo", and culminated in Janan's withdrawal from the
League of Nations. It is probable that this "independent
diplomacy" will continue, but its manifestations will
presumably

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
presumably not be so obvious and blatant as they were
during Shiratori's regime as Chief of the Bureau of
Information and Intelligence. The attitude of the
Foreign Office towards the United States can be expected
to be improved by Shiratori's removal. Shiratori was
in the habit of assisting in the propaganda of his
military friends by giving publicity to absurd rumors
about American assistance to China; on one occasion even
accusing the American Government of being ignorant of the
actions of American officials in China. He carried this
propaganda to such lengths that he ac uired the reputa-
tion of being intensely anti-American, but it is doubtful
if he really is as anti-American as he seems. His love
of sensation has probably played a part in his utterances.
At any rate, it is to be honed that his removal will mean
a cessation of anti-American nronaganda emanating from the
Foreign Office.
The foreign press representatives in Tokyo are not
particularly pleased with the removal of Shiratori to the
field. Shiratori could usuelly be counted unon to furnish
them with interesting copy ind, moreover, he frecuently
told them the blunt truth, without regard to the usual
diplomatic subterfuges :nd without consideration for the
feelings of the J pan ese field officers, who may have been
assuring the overuments to vhicn they were accredited of
the exact oooosite of Shiratori's statements. The press
corresponcents fear that the removal of Shiratori will mean
a return to the former nolicy of evasiveness nd subterfuge in
the relations of the Foreign Office with the press. On the
other

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.95)
-8-
other hand, these same correspondents have sometimes com-
plained that they have been placed in embarrassing positions
by some of Shiratori's more blatant utterances and especially
by his occasional fantastic charges of hostile actions or
intentions on the part of foreign nations, particularly
2
the United States. While knowing these charges to be utterly
false and possibly mischievous, the correspondents have been
obliged to cable them to their agencies or papers lest they
be admonished for failing to cover the news.
Mr. Eiji Amau, now Counsellor of Embassy at Moscow, is
reported to be slated to succeed Shiratori at the Bureau of
Information and Intelligence of the Foreign Office. Mr.
Srburo Kurusu, now Director of the Bureau of Commercial Affairs
of the Foreign Office, is also mentioned for the post.
Resoectfully yours,
Soog

July 26, 1934.
The allotment for the fisoal year
ended Maroh 31, 1934, of the Department
of Foreign Affairs of the Japanese
Government, consisted of Yen 16,326,000
for ordinary expenditures and of Yen
10,063,000 for extraordinary expenditures,
a total of about Yen 26,400.000, the
•quivalent of about $15,200,000at the
former normal rate of exchange, or
about $8,500,000 at the present rate of
exohange.
Of the ordinary expenditures,
Yen 3,600,000 was set aside for the
operation of the Foreign Office.
In the extraordinary expenditures
there was included an item of Yen 2,300ρ00
for speciall allowances to offset
the depreciation of the Yen.
The total appropriations for the
Department of State for the present fisoal
year is about $13,500,000, I believe. Taking
into account the fact that the Japanese
Foreign Service, both in point of personnel
and mstablishments, is less than half the
side of our service, the surprise felt by
the Foreign Office is understandable.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 25, 1934.
J
This report from the Embassy on changes
in the organization of the Japanese Foreign
Office is interestingly written and well
worth reading.
There are several features of the
reorganization of the Foreign Office as
described in the enclosure which should be
notod.
Hitherto, the "senior" burean was the
Asia Bureau, which covered Rusasia and
the Near East as well as Asia. The other
geographical bureau covered the rest of the
world, and it was labelled the "European-
American Bureau". The First Section of the
Asia Bureau dealt with China alone, while
the First Section of the European-American
Bureau dealt with European affairs. By and
large the chiefs of the European-American Bureau
were men more with European experience than
with American experience. It is interesting
to observe how the emphasis has been shifted
in the new set-up.
The new Eastern Asia Bureau is in effect
the First Section of the old Asia Burean, set
up as an independent diwision, plus the
section brought over from the Commercial
Bureau which dealt with "protection" cases.
The new European and Asiatic Bureau quite
obviously considers relations with Russia as

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
its most important business, while relations
with Britain, France and the League, around
which the old European-American Bureau used
to revolve, have been relegated to a position
of minor importanoe. Again, ralations with
the United States, which were considered to
he secandary to relations with Western
Enrapean aauntries, have been entrusted to
an independent bureau.
Those changes indioate rather strikingly
the recent re-orientation of Japan's foreign
polioies.

in the form of an appendix hereto an outline of the organiz
tion and functions of the Foreign Office, as revised receht
by Imperial Ordinance. In a separate despatch (No. 877, of
July 4, 1934) is transmitted a list of the officers of the
Foreign Office and of the Japanese Embassies, Legations and
Consulates throughout the world, compiled as of June 1, 1934.
F/ESP
The Japanese Foreign Office has suffered considerable
loss of prestige and even authority through the events of the
past

=== Page 187 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
past few years. Popular resentment at what was considered
Japanese failure at the London Naval Conference, and at the
"weak-kneed" China policy of Baron Shidehara, had much to do
with the success of the military in breaking away from civi-
lian control in the Manchurian affair. With the passing of
Shidehara came the report of the Lytton Commission of Inquiry,
the vote of the League censuring Japan, and the decision of
Japan to withdraw from the League. Many Japanese felt that
the situation had been clumsily handled by the Foreign Office;
that Yoshizawa, the Japanese representative at Geneva, was a
bungler, and that his successor, Matsuoka, was a braggart.
It was felt that if the Foreign Office had been more adroit
in presenting Japan's views, Japan would not have been forced
to withdraw from the League and would not now be faced by
a hostile world intent on restricting Japanese commercial
expansion.
More recently, the result of the Indo-Japanese trade ne-
gotiations in which the Japanese consider themselves to have been
worsted, called down fresh obloquy on the Foreign Office. During
the Diet session early this year much criticism was directed at
rule-of-thunb diplomacy, of the ineptness of the Foreign Office,
and of the poor personnel of the Japanese Foreign Service. The
press, possibly instigated from military quarters, loses no op-
portunity to flay the know-nothing diplomats, who have often had
to take the blame for, and attempt to settle, the situations
created without their knowledge by the military.
Much of this criticism is obviously unjust. Under the
Japanese Constitution each Minister of State is independent
in his own sphere, and rosponsible only to the Emperor.
However

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
However, it is only in Foreign Affairs that concessions
must be made to views differing from the accepted Japanese
view. It is only in Foreign Affairs that the accepted
national policy is subject to reversal, and reversals are
never pleasing to an insular and intensely nationalistic
people. Consequently the Minister for Foreign Affairs is
probably more subject to interference from his colleagues
and criticism by the public than any other minister of state.
Moreover, the Foreign Office is severely handicapped in
finances and equipment. The Gaimusho is housed in disgraceful
old shacks, contrasting strikingly with the fine new quarters
of other departments. The total appropriation for the Gaimusho
is about Yen 25,000,000, in contrast to the Yen 900,000,000
of the Army and Navy. The pay is exceedingly low, except for
officers stationed abroad who are given generous living allow-
ances, and promotion is considerably slower than in other
branches of the government service.
Except for a man of exceptional personality, the port-
folio of Foreign Affairs has no authority comparable to that
wielded by the American Secretary of State. The Foreign
Minister has no powerful backing by a president or king; he
may have the sympathy of the Imperial Court, but his advice
to the Emperor may be completely over-ridden by the military
who not only have closer access to the Sovereign through the
Imperial Princes, but who are also practically independent
of all civilian control. The Minister for Foreign Affairs
must frame his policies in deference to the opinions of his
colleagues; must attempt to carry them out in a world often
unsympathetic to Japanese aims; must be prepared against
independent

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
independent actions on the part of the military, and must
take the blame for all failures in the conduct of foreign
affairs. Truly his lot is not easy.
The present Foreign Minister, Mr. Hirota, a man of un-
usual personality and determination, has managed to restore
much prestige to the Foreign Office, and more important, has
succeeded in establishing a definite foreign policy of peace
and conciliation in place of the dangerously truculent atti-
tude of his predecessor. The Embassy has on several occasions
discussed Mr. Hirota and his policies. The changes in the
Foreign Office organization, mentioned above, have been ad-
vocated for some time, both by Mr. Hirota and his predecessor,
Count Uchida. While these changes are not particularly
important, they appear to be part of a general effort to
render the Gaimusho more efficient and better able to meet
the increased demands on Japanese diplomacy, particularly
in commercial affairs.
The chief change which appears in the organization of
the Foreign Office is the division of the old Bureau of Eu-
ropean and American Affairs (O-Bei Kyoku) into a Bureau of
European and (western) Asiatic Affairs, and a Bureau of
American Affairs. The latter is headed by Mr. Kensuke Hori-
nouchi, former counselor of the Embassy in Washington, and
the first section, dealing with North American Affairs, is
headed by Mr. Tatsuki Sakamoto, one time secretary of the
Washington Embassy.
Earlier this year a new Bureau of Investigation was
added to the Foreign Office, in response to a demand from a
certain

=== Page 190 === (confidence 1.00)
-5-
certain section of the Privy Council. The idea seems to
have been to establish a kind of general staff for. the Foreign
Office, where the whole field of diplomacy might be studied
and broad policies in foreign affairs mapped out. However,
the Investigation Bureau as established seems to be an unwanted
stepchild, with no particular function and little authority.
Mr. Horinouchi, the chief, has recently been appointed director
of the Bureau of American Affairs "in addition to his present
duties".
The transfer of passport and immigration matters from
the Commercial Bureau to the new American Bureau leaves the
former with its staff largely intact. There are plans on
foot, now being discussed in connection with preparation of
the new budget estimates, for closer cooperation between the
Foreign Office and the Department of Commerce and Industry in
the matter of foreign trade promotion and protection. There
seems to be some duplication of work and jealousy between these
two departments comparable to similar situations existing in
certain other countries. In any event an effort is to be made
to render the Foreign Office more efficient in handling com-
mercial matters in these times of keen international trade
rivalries.
Respectfully yours,
Enclosure:
As stated above.

Functions of Bureaus and Sections in
the Department of Foreign Affairs, as
reorganized June, 1934.
In the Foreign Minister's secretariat are established
the following sestions:
1. The Section of Personnel and Protocol handles
the following business: personnel matters of officers
and clerks in the service of the Department; credentials,
letters of recall; letters of attorney; exequaturs and
certificates; decorations and medals; audience and
other ceremonies; examinations for the foreign service.
2. The Section of Archives handles the following
matters: receipt, distribution and delivery of documents;
statistical reports and official announcements for
publication in the Official Gazette; custody of official
seals and certificates; certification of official pspers;
classification and preservation of documents and books;
publication of diplomatic documents.
3. The Section of Accounts conducts the following
business: budget estimates; settled accounts and other
accounts; supervision and control of accounts; State
properties and articles belonging to the Department;
engagement, dismissal and control of employees.
4. The Telegraph Section attends to the receipt and
despatch of telegrams, and prepares codes.
Bureau.of Eastern Asiatic Affairs:
lst Section: China, Hongkong, Macao.
2nd Section: Protection of Japanese residents abroad,
and relations with Siam.
3rd Section: Manchukuo.
Bureau of European and Asiatic Affairs:
1st Section: Soviet Russia, Finland, Estonia, Latvia,
Lithuania, Poland, Turkey, Persia and Afghan-
istan.
2nd Section: Affairs of countries other than those
mentioned above.
Bureau of American Affairs.
lst Section: Canada, United States of America and
dependencies.
2nd Section: All countries of America other than those
above-mentioned.
3rd Section: Passport matters.
Bureau of Commercial Affairs:
lst Section: Policies and systems of commerce and navigation
2nd Section: Protection, promotion and regulation of foreign
trade and navigation.
3rd Section: Other commercial affairs, trade reports,etc.
Bureau of Treatie

=== Page 192 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
Bureau of Treaties:
lst Section: Affairs related to the conclusion, inter-
pretation, ratification, publication and com-
pilation of treaties and other international
agreements.
2nd Section: Affairs related to legal matters of inter-
national concern; international administrative
matters.
Bureau of Information (properly a sub-bureau)
lst Section: Newspaper and newsagency matters.
2nd Section: Other matters not attended to by the lst
Section.
3rd Section: General affairs.
Bureau of Cultural Undertakings (properly a sub-bureau)
1st Section: Cultural undertakings in China.
2nd Section: General affairs of the Bureau.
Bureau of Investigation (properly a sub-bureau)
1st Section: Research and study of diplomatic history,
and matters not under control of other sections.
2nd Section: Arrangement of records; recording of the
proceedings of international negotiations.
3rd Section: Investigation of political diplomatic, com-
mercial and economic matters relating to
Manchukuo and China.
4th Section: Investigation of political, diplomatic,
commercial and economic affairs relating to
European countries.
5th Section: Investigation of political, diplomatic,
commercial and economic affairs relating to
the countries of North, Gentral and South
America.

There are presented hereunder certain comparative'
figures relating to appropriations, salaries and allowances
of the American Foreign Service and of the Japanese Foreign
25
Service, respectively.
The present study was inspired by a despatch (No. 854
894.021/45
of June 22, 1934) from the American Embassy at Tokyo, which
transmitted a story to the effect that the Japanese Foreign
Office had instructed the Embassy at Washington to report
the appropriations of the Department with a view to use in
support of the demands of the Foreign Office for increased
appropriations; that the Embassy at Washington had obtained
the desired figures and transmitted them to the Foreign
Office; that the Foreign Cffice believed "that there must
894.021
obviously be some mistake as the figures were far too low";
and that the appropriations of the Department could not be
NOV 30.1935.
used to support the demand of the Foreign Office for
FILED
increased appropriations.
Although there is no provision of law stipulating that
the Minister for Foreign Affairs and ambassadors shall be
selected from the Foreign Service, the entire foreign
F
relations organization of the Japanese Government is a
FG
career

=== Page 195 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
organization. The Minister for Foreign Affairs and the
Vice-Minister and all the ambassadors and ministers are
"graduates" of the Foreign Service.
The Japanese Foreign Service has a total personnel
of 440 officers, including the Minister for Foreign Affairs,
the Vice-Minister for Foreign Affairs, ten ambassadors
en poste and four on the waiting list, and twenty ministers.
The higher officials of the Foreign Service, with the excep-
tion of a few technical experts and permanent officials,
consist entirely of members of the Foreign Service, the
latest Foreign Office List indicating that there are
assigned to duty at Tokyo ll3 members of the Foreign Service.

The allotment for the fiscal year, ending March 31,
1934, of the Department of Foreign Affairs of the Japanese
Government was Yen 26,400,000, the equivalent of about
*13,200,000 at the former normal rate of exchange, or
about 8,500,000 at the present rate of exchange. The total
appropriation for the Department of State for the present
fiscal year is about 13,500,000. However, in analyzing
the various items making up the total appropriation, and
in comparing those items with corresponding items in the
appropriation for the Department of State, it is necessary
to take into consideration the following facts:
(a) Many of the allotments to the Foreign
Office do not exactly correspond with any allot-
ments to the Department of State;
(b) The proportion of officers stationed at
home is larger in the case of the Japanese Foreign
Service than in the case of the American Foreign
Service. The various allowances that are available
for higher o ficers in the field are, therefore,
distributed among relatively and actually fewer
persons;
(c) Although

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
(c) Although the cost in Japanese currency
of maintaining missions and consulates abroad
has been increased by the decline in the exchange
value of Japanese currency, costs and prices in
Japan have not been substantially increased by
unfavorable exchange rates.
The allowances for the Foreign Office have, in view
of sub-paragraph (c) above, been reduced in this memorandum
to dollars at the former normal rate of exchange of
approximately two Yen for one dollar, while allotments and
allowances for use in the field have been calculated, except
in those cases where provision has been made for loss by
exchange, at the current rate of exohange, which is:
1 Yen - $0.30.
Operation and Maintenance. The Japanese Government
owns the embassy buildings at Paris, Berlin and Washington
and the legation buildings at Peiping. The Japanese Embassy
at London, which is one of the finest at that capital, is
held under a long-term leasehold. The Japanese Government
also owns consular buildings at twenty-eight posts, twenty-
five of these being situated in China.
At posts where the Japanese Government does not own
official buildings, living quarters are provided and main-
tained for the principal officers, whether they be ambassadors,
ministers, consuls general or consuls.

of the American and Japanese Governments, respectively, for

Salaries and Allowances. The entire Japanese Civil
Service is a classified service, with a schedule of graduated
salaries applicable to officers and employees of all depart-
ments: an official of a certain grade receives the anpointed
salary for that grace regardless of whether he be a member of
the Foreign Office or of any other government department.
This schedule of salaries is extremely low.
The Civil Service is divided into two parts: "the
higher civil service" and "the lower civil service". Above
the Civil Service are those high officials of the Japanese
Government, such as cabinet officers, ambassadors, governors
general of colonies and full admirals and generals, who are
entitled to receive their commissions directly from the
Emperor.
There

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
There is attached a schedule of salaries paid to
members of the Japanese Foreign Service. The lower civil
service comprises clerks, consular police (of which there
are a considerable number in China) and custodial employees.
The salaries set forth in the schedule are the only
emoluments paid to officers when stationed in Japan or
en disponibilité, other than allowances accruing to them by
virtue of holding court decorations. When on post, however,
all officers receive at least one allowance, known as a
"representation allowance". This is not a post allowance.
It is intended to make good the difference between the cost
of living by Japanese standards and by occidental standards
and to permit officers in the field to maintain a standard
of living equivalent to that maintained by their Western
colleagues. The allowance fixed for the officers at each
post is, however, determined to some degree by the cost of
living at that post and by its relative importance. There
1s attached a schedule of representation allowances at the
missions and at several of the more important consular
posts. The figures set forth in that schedule have been
converted from Japanese currency at the normal rate of
exchange, as provision is made for loss by exchange in
respect of representation allowances.
Officers stationed abroad, who are accompanied by
their families, receive a "family allowance" of 40 per cent
of their representation allowance.
The

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.98)
-7 -
The Japanese Government provides and maintains at
each mission and consulate one or more automobiles for
the use of the principal officers.
Chiefs of mission, diplomatic counselors and prin-
cipal consular officers are granted allowances for enter-
tainment. The amounts are not specified and are granted
by the Government to meet the normal requirements of the
various offices, supplemented by additional allowances
to meet extraordinary needs. The total appropriation is
very small -- about $90,000, but there is available the
secret fund of the Foreign Office of about $750,000 which
may be drawn upon for this purpose.
There are attached tables of comparative figures of
salaries and allowances received by American and Japanese
officers at certain typical posts. They indicate that
senior officers in the Japanese service receive substantially
more than do their American colleagues, the difference in
salary and allowances in the case of ambassadors being about
13,O00, in addition to perquisites the value of which cannot
be exactly stated, and in the case of the salary and allowances
of consuls being about 2,200, also in addition to certain
perquisites. In the case of junior officers, the difference
is not very large, but here again the Japanese officer has
the advantage.

=== Page 208 === (confidence 1.00)
A large room in the Gaimusho, called the Hikaye Shitsu,
or Interviewers' Room, is reserved for newspaper men. It
adjoins the office of the Bu-cho. As in the United States,
representatives of the press have almost constant access to
the publicity agent of the foreign office. But the Japanese
have not followed American practice in another respect.
The American Secretary of State holds weekly, and sometimes
daily, conferences with the press, actually permitting
questions to be asked orally by representatives of both
native and foreign newspapers. The Japanese minister of
foreign affairs stands more aloof. His office is not even
in the main building of the Gaimusho, but in his official
residence on the opposite side of the compound. Distinguished
newspaper-men, singly, by appointment, may often secure
interviews with the minister, but seldom in a group.
(From chapter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
The second service is the Bunka Jygyo-bu or Cultural
Relations Bcard, originally established in l9l3, and charged
with the duty of promoting goodwill and Japanese influence
in foreign countries.1 The board is headed by a Bu-cho
of Chokunin rank. In another place we will discuss the
propaganda methods of the Japanese Government. Suffice it
here to say that Japan has followed European practice by the
delegation of this function to one of the units of the foreign
office. Finally, the third service is the Chasabu or
Investigation Service, divided into five sections, and
charged with the duty of carrying on investigations of
1. Created by Imperial (rdinance Mo. 527 of 1923.
Genko Horei Shuran (1927), Vo1. I, bk. 1i1, p.14.
2. See the Kwampo, January 6, 1934.
(From chapter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
According to general belief, the Denshin-ka follows
the usual European practice (and the practice of the American
Government from 1917 to 1929) of purloining the telegraphic
messages of foreign states.1 It is never difficult for the
Telegraph Service to secure the contents of any message sent
by a foreign government through Japanese telegraph and
cable companies. Such messages, in the hands of the decoding
experts, who are, of course, under the necessity of
reconstructing the foreign code, are patiently turned into
Japanese words. Under French instructors, even before the
World War, Japanese officials made remarkable progress in
the art of deciphering.
1. The American office charged with this not highly
honorable task was abolished by Secretary Stimson
in 1929, while the discharged chief of this
service took his revenge upon the upright Secretary
by exposing the former practice. See Herbert 0.
Yardley, The American Black Chamber (Indianapolis,
1931).
(From chanter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
The statesmen of the Meiji era learned the lesson
which Germany and England in the nineteenth century were
best prepared to teach -- the lesson that efficient
administration requires a highly qualified personnel, and
that such a personnel can be obtai ned only by skilful means
of recruitment. The Foreign Service is a human machine.
Of all the governmental branches, because of its varied
contacts with foreign countries, this department stands
in the most need of a uniformly superior personnel. And
thus, the recruitment of its officers is a question to
which the Japanese Government has given more than usual
attentlon.
(From chapter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
As a.result of these modifications in the Yamagata
ordinances, the civil service contains a considerable number
of permanent appointees who have escaped the competitive
examinations. They can be found in every department with
the single exception of the Foreign Office. Because of
the imperative requirement of a mastery of Languages, and
because of a characteristic Japanese pride which demands
a superior personnel in the department which represents
Nippon in foreign affairs, the permanent posts in the
Foreign Office have not been thrown open to politicians,
with the result that the competitive examination remains
as the only gateway to a career in the Foreign Service.
(From chapter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
Rarely is a career officer left at any assignment
for more than three years.
(From chapter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 214 === (confidence 1.00)
A few words regarding the salaries will not be out
of place. Undoubtedly the Foreign Service is better paid
than are the other governmental services. This does not
apply to the officers in Tokyo, for the same salary scale is
attached to officers of similar rank in every department.
But, to the officers in the field, generous service
allowances are paid which not only cover official expenses
but also even some of the household charges which must be
borne by Tokyo officers out of their own salaries.
Ambassadors receive an allowance that runs as high as 45,000
yen per year. Even consuls, in inland cities receive
allowances that permit them to maintain establishments as
dignified, if not as luxurious, as successful businessmen.
Even during the economic depression in the post-war period,
allowances were not cut, a fact that together with
excellent methods of recruitment and administration permits
the personnel of the Japanese foreign service to maintain a
high standard of competence.
("rom chanter on "Japanese Foreign Office" by
Kenneth Colegrove.)

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.99)
Unquestionably, the foreign service of the Japanese
Empire, in comparison with the other Great Powers, ranks
high. If it is not the equal of the British and the French
systems, it is certainly superior to the service of the
United States and of most other countries. Japan early
learned the lesson that a good diplomat is worth several
battle-ships. Its foreign service to-day includes a
considerable variety of well-trained and experienced
agents, loyal to the Imperial tradition, and alert to
promote every interest of Nippon.
(From Chapter on "Japanese Foreign Cffice" by Kenneth Colegrove.)

The Administrative Vice Minister of Foreign Affairs has,

tions between Japan and the United States. These relations
70219411
are dealt with in the first instanoe by the Bureau of
American Affairs, which is divided into three sections.
894.021/48
The first section deals with the United States, the Philip-
pine Islands and Canada. This section employs, besides the
chief, one officer and four clerks. The second section
deals with all matters concerning Latin America, and employs,
besides the chief, two officers and seven clerks. The third
section is in charge of all immigration matters as they
affect foreign relations.
Important matters affecting commercial relations be-
tween Japan and the United States are dealt with, in the
first instance, by the Bureau of Commercial Affairs which
consists, besides the chief, of a secretariat of five
officers and three sections employing a total of sixteen
officers. It may be stated that the Japanese Foreign Office
has on foot a plan to expand the Commercial Bureau and to
F/F
give
G

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
give it enlarged powers to make decisions in regard to trade
and tariff matters.
Japan maintains in the United States an embassy, three
consulates general and five consulates. In these there are
on duty a total of thirty-two career officers. There are
also five honorary consuls located in large cities. In
addition a vice consul is maintained in Panama, C.Z.; there
is a consulate general in Manila, P.I.; and a vice consul
at Davao, P.I. There is also a honorary consul in San Juan,
Puerto Rico, although the Japanese Consul General at New
York has supervision over Japanese affairs in that island.
Salaries paid to Japanese officers are much lower than
those paid American officers of equal rank, but each Japa-
nese officer receives a substantial "representation allow-
ance". Principal Japanese officers also receive entertain-
ment allowances, the amounts of which are not known. In
addition, Japan maintains living quarters for the principal
officers at all Japanese consulates in the United States.
An automobile is provided for each principal officer.
It is safe to say that Japan spends at least $215,000
a year on salaries and "representation allowances" of her
diplomatic officers in the United States. Moreover, it is
known that the secret fund of the Japanese Foreign Office
amounting to $750,000 annually, is drawn upon to employ an
unlnown

=== Page 232 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
unkmown number of American citizens in the United States
who act as advisers and propagandists for the Japanese
Government.
In the State Department the agency primarily concerned
with Japanese Affairs is the Division of Far Eastern Affairs,
headed at present by a chief and two assistant chiefs.
Under them, dealing exclusively with Japanese affairs, are
two Foreign Service officers assisted by one clerk.
In the Japanese Empire the United States maintains an
embassy, two consulates general and six consulates, employ-
ing all together twenty-six career officers. The United
States owns a building in Tokyo which provides offices for
all American Government agencies, quarters for clerks and
State Department language officers, and a residence for the
Ambassador. At four of the six consulates the United States
owns or leases buildings which serve as offices and living
quarters for the principal officers.
While salaries paid to American Foreign Service officers
are much larger than those paid by Japan to her officers,
the allowances which supplement the incomes are much lower
than those which Japan pays. In the case of principal
officers who live in combined quarters, there is at times
no additional allowance paid whatsoever. American Foreign
Service officers receive no entertainment allowance, nor
does the American Government provide automobiles for them.
The total amount of money spent by the American Government
for

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
for salaries and allowances of consular and diplomatio
officers in Japan is approximately $140,000 a year, almost
$75,000 a year less than Japan pays to her Foreign Office
representatives in the United States. When the amount
spent by Japan in the United States for entertainment allow-
ances, and the money obtained from the secret fund of the
Japanese Foreign Office are added, it may be estimated that
Japan spends well over $100,000 a year more in the United
States than the United States spends in Japan.
Mention should be made in this connection of several
agencies maintained by Japan in the United States, which are
not directly connected with the Foreign Office. The Japanese
Army, Navy, Department of Finance, Government Railway Office,
Taiwan Government General and the South Manchuria Railway
maintain agents in this country.
It thus appears that in the number of offices maintained
by the Japanese Foreign Office in the United States and by
the State Department in Japan, as well as in the number of
officers attached thereto, there is no great difference.
However, in the facilities granted to officers for carrying
on their work, Japan has a decided advantage. There are
appended hereto comparative tables of the remuneration of
the staffs of American and Japanese diplomatic and consular
establishments in the two countries.

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
In an endeavor to assess the importanoe placed on
relations with each other by Japan and the United States
a study has been made of the agencies maintained by each
country for the conduct of relations with the other. The
present memorandum deals primarily with the agencies main-
tained by the Japanese Foreign Office and the United States
Department of State, respectively, and only lists in out-
line those maintained by other branches of the Government.
The results of the study show that in the number of
offices maintained by the Japanese Foreign Office in the
United States and by the State Department in Japan, as
well as in the number of officers attached thereto, there
is not a large difference between the two countries, but
in the facilities granted to officers for carrying on their
work, Japan has a deoided advantage.
In the Japanese Foreign Office, as now organized
(March 1935), Japan's relations with the United States
are dealt with in the first instance by the Bureau of
American Affairs, the director of which is an officer
who at one time served as Japanese Consul General in
New York City. The Bureau is divided into three sec-
tions. The first section deals exclusively with the
United

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
一3-
United States; and it consists of a seotion chief and
two assistants, all of whom have had experience as dip-
lomatio or consular officers in the United States. The
second section deals with all matters affecting Japan's
relations with Latin America. The third seotion is in
charge of all immigration mattere as they affeot foreign
relations.
Important matters affeoting commeroial relations
between Japan and the United States are dealt with, in
the first instance, by the Bureau of Commercial Affairs,
whose chief is a high ranking officer in the Japanese
diplomatic service and who has served many years in the
United States. This Bureau consists of a secretariat
of five officers and three sections employing a total of
sixteen officers, many of whom have lived in the United
States. The Commercial Seoretary of the Japanese Embassy
in Washington is a direot representative of this Bureau.
The Administrative Vice Minister of Foreign Affairs
hes, under the Foreign Minister, general supervision over
matters affecting relations between the two countries.
Japan maintains in the United States an embassy,
three consulates general, and five consulates. In these
there are on duty a total of thirty-two career officers.
In addition there are five honorary consuls located in
large

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
一3-
large cities. The above figures include the Hawaiian
Islands but do not include the Philippine Islands or
the Canal Zone. A vice consul is maintained at Panama,
C. Z.; there is a consulate general in Manila, P. I.;
and there is a vice consul, who is acting consul, at
Davao, P. I. There is also an honorary consul in San
Juan, Puerto Rico, although the Japanese Consul General
in New York has supervision over Japanese affairs in
that island.
Offices maintained by Japan in the United States
and the Territory of Hawaii are as follows: Embassy,
Washington; consulates general, New York, San Francisco,
Honolulu, T. H.; consulates, Seattle, Portland, Los
Angeles, New Orleans,and Chicago; honorary consulates,
Mobile, Juneau (Alaska), Boston, Philadelphia, and
Galveston.
Salaries paid to Japanese officers are much lower
than those paid American officers of equal rank, but
each Japanese officer receives a substantial "represen-
tation allowance". Principal Japanese officers also
receive entertainment allowances the amounts of which
are not known. In addition Japan maintains living
quarters for the principal officers at all Japanese
consulates in the United States as well as for the
Counselor of Embassy in Washington. The Japanese
Embassy

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
Embassy buildings in Washington are owned by the Imperial
Government and provide quarters for the Ambassador as
well as office rooms. The Japanese Ambassador in Wash-
ington is also provided with an automobile and chauffeur.
The "representation allowances" differ from country
to country and from post to post within a country de-
pending upon the cost of living and the estimated im-
portance of the post. It is interesting to note that
the Japanese Ambassador in Washington receives the largest
"representation allowance" granted to any Japanese Ambas-
sador abroad. It is impossible to obtain exact figures
showing the amount of money spent by Japan in maintain-
ing her diplomatic and consular establishments in the
United States, but it is possible to make a substantially
accurate estimate based on the salaries and "representa-
tion allowances" paid. It is safe to say that Japan
spends at least $215,000 a year on these two items alone.
It is known that the secret fund of the Japanese
Foreign Office amounting to approximately $750,000
annually, is drawn upon to employ an unknown number of
American citizens, in the United States, who aot as
advisers to and propagandists for the Japanese Govern-
ment.
There are attached two tables showing the salaries
and allowances received by the Japanese Ambassador and
his staff in Washington, and the American Ambassador and
h18

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5-
his staff in Tokyo, as well as the remuneration received
by the staffs of the Japanese Consulate General in New
York and the American Consulate General in Tokyo. The
amount spent as entertainment allowance can only be
estimated but it is believed that a total of at least
35,000 a year is spent by the Japanese Ambassador in
Washington and the Consuls General in New York and San
Francisoo.
Department of State Agencies Dealing with Japanese Affairs.
In the State Department the agenoy primarily con-
cerned with Japanese affairs is the Division of Far Eastern
Affairs, headed at present by a chief and two assistant
chiefs all of whom have lived in the Far East. Under them,
dealing exclusively with Japanese affairs, are two Foreign
Service officers who have lived many years in Japan and
are thoroughly conversant with its language, oustoms and
history. The Under Secretary of State, under the Secretary
of State, exercises general supervision over all matters
involving relations of Japan and the United States.
In the Japanese Empire the United States maintains
an Embassy, two consulates general and six consulates,
employing altogether twenty-six career officers. The
United States does not have any honorary consuls or
consular agents in the Japanese Empire. The United
States

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.98)
一6-
States owne the Embassy building in Tokyo, which provides
offices for all American Government agencies, quarters
for clerks and State Department language officers, and
a residence for the Ambassador. At four of the sir con-
sulates the American Government owns or leases buildings
whioh serve as offices and living quarters for the prin-
cipal officer.
While salaries paid to American Foreign Service
officers are much larger than those paid by Japan to
her officers, the allowances which supplement the in-
come are not as large as those which Japan pays. In
the case of principal officers who live in combined
quarters, there is at times no additional allowance
paid whatsoever. American Foreign Service officers
reoeive no entertainment allowance, nor does the
American Government provide an automobile for the Am-
bassador in Tokyo. The total amount of money spent by
the Amerioan Government for salaries and allowances of
her consular and diplomatio officers in Japan is but
slight1y more than $140,000 a year, almost $75,000 a year
less than Japan pays in the United States to her Foreign
Office representatives. When the amount spent by Japan
in the United States for entertainment allowances, and
the money obtained from the secret fund in the Japanese
Foreign

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
Foreign Office are added, it may be estimated that Japan
spends well over $100,000 a year more in the United States
than the United States spends in Japan.
Other Government Agenoies.
Japan also maintains in the United States at least
six government agenoies which are not direotly connected
with the Foreign Office. These are military and naval
establishments, financial commissioner, Japanese Govern-
ment railways office, Taiwan (Formosa) Government General,
and the office of the South Manchurian Railways, the last
named of whioh in addition to its normal activities engages
in distribution of propaganda for "Manchukuo".
The only one of the above agencies for which there
is immediately available any detailed information as to
their staffs is that maintained by the Navy Department.
In Washington Japan has a naval attachd and two assistant
naval attaohds. In New York there are ten officers
attached to the naval supply office and there are also
seven Japanese naval language officers. All of Japan's
naval offioers in the United States are of the rank of
lieutenant commander or above.
It is believed that the Japanese military establish-
ment in the United States is at least equal to that of the
navy. There is a a military attache attached to the
Japanese Embassy and he has two assistants. There are
also

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
also seven language officers. The military attachd holds
the rank of colonel and one of his assistants is a major,
the other a captain.
It has not been possible as yet to obtain any infor-
mation as to salaries or allowances paid these officers
but it is known that both the military and naval attaches
are furnished with substantial entertainment allowances
and all officers have a good-sized fund to be used for
travel.
In Japan the United States has a military and naval
attache, each of which has one assistant. There are seven
army and seven naval officers assigned to Japan for
language study. Both of the attaches are provided with
motor cars by their respective Departments and both also
have funds for entertainment.
The Department of Commerce maintains an office in
Tokyo which has a staff of three officers, a commercial
attache, a trade commissioner and an assistant trade
commissioner. In Kobe the Treasury Department maintains
a treasury attache and one assistant attachd and there
was formerly a representative of the Department of Agri-
culture stationed in Japan. At present the agriculture
commissioner in Shanghai makes trips to Japan from time
to time for investigation.

B. Japan Plans to Augment Trade Envoys
Large increases in the personnel devoted exclusively
to the handling of commercial affairs in Japanese embassies,
logations and consular establishments were recommended on
29 May to Foreign Minister Hirota by the "Efficiency
Commission" of his ministry.
Under the draft plan submitted by the commission two
additional counsellors, five secretaries, seven consuls,
nine vice-consuls and four chancellors will be assigned to
various posts throughout the world to deal with matters of
international trade.
The Japanese enbassies at Washington and Changchun will
each be assigned a new commercial counsellor while five new
commercial secretaries will be attached to the embassies and
legations in France, the United States, Manchoukuo, Canada
and Siam.
The seven newly created commercial consuls will be
attached to the consulates-general at San Francisco, Sydney,
Shanghai, Tsingtao, Nanking, Amoy, and Canton, while the new
vice-consuls will be detailed to Singapore, Manila, Hankow,
Hongkong, Harbin, Mukden, Panama, Bombay, and Soerabaya.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 5, 1936.
Tokyo's attached despatch No. 1623,
January 7, 1936, reports a private con-
versation between Dickover and Kurusu of
the Japanese Foreign Office with regard
to reports of impending changes in the
Japanese Foreign Service. Kurusu said
that Arita was not willing to become
Ambassador to China if the Japanese
military continued to dominate Japanese
diplomacy. The outcome being uncertain
nothing was yet decided as to Kurusu's
going to Belgium as Ambassador (Arita's
present assignment). Kurusu said that
Horinouchi would become vice minister
when Shigemitsu goes to the field again
as ambassador. Kurusu said that Debuchi
will surely be foreign minister sometime.

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. l to despatch
No. 1623 of January 7, 1936,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
MEMORANDUM.
January 6, 1936.
Conversation: Mr. S. Kurusu, Chief of the Bureau of Com-
mercial Affairs, Japanese Foreign Ofrice.
Mr. Dickover, of the Embassy.
In the course of a conversation last evening with Mr.
Kurusu, I asked if anything had been decided as yet in re-
gard to his rumored transfer to Belgium as Japanese Ambas-
sador. He replied that nothing was decided definitely as
yet, because Mr. Arita (former Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs, now Japanese Ambassador to Belgium, and reported
reliably to be the choice of the Foreign Office for the
next Japanese Ambassador to China) was feeling around to
ascertain his exact position. He did not, Mr. Kurusu said,
wish to have to serve several masters if he goes to China
as Ambassador. I said that I assumed that by that Mr. Kurusu
meant that Mr. Arita was not willing to serve as Japanese
Ambassador in China if the Japanese military continued to
dominate Japanese diplomacy there. Kurusu admitted that that
was the point.
Later the conversation touched on the choice of the next
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs, if and when, as is reported,
Mr. Shigemitsu is sent out to the field again as Ambassador.
I said that I had heard that Togo (Chief of the Bureau of
European and Asiatic Affairs) and Horinouchi (Chief of the
Bureau of American Affairs) were both mentioned for the post.
Mr. Kurusu said that Mr. Horinouchi would get the post. He
said that Horinouchi was a slow, cuiet man, but very sound and
reliable.
Later the conversation turned to Mr. Debuchi, former Ambas-
sador to the United States. Mr. Kurusu said that Mr. Debuchi
would surely "come back" and that he would be Minister for
Foreign Affairs.
E.R.D.

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.98)
(a).
The Japanese Foreign Service.
It was stated by the TOKYO NICHI NICHI of September 11
that as a result of pressure from younger diplomats and
outside sources as well the Foreign Office was contemplating
a thorough-going shift of Japanese chiefs of mission, em-
bracin the retirement of the following persons: Mr.
Naotake Sato, Ambassador to France; Viscount Kintomo
Mushakoji, Ambassador to Germany; Mr. Iemasa Tokugawa,
Ambassador to Turkey; Mr. Eisuke Fujita, Minister to
Rumania; MxxxXasukiehi.XatakexfexmerxMinistexxtmxSiam;
Mr. Akio Kasama, Minister to Portugal; Mr. Takezo Okamoto,
Minister to Persia; and Mr. Jiro Yamazaki, Minister to
Argentina. According to the TOKYO NICHI NICHI, it is
expected that Lr. Yotaro Sugimura, Ambassador to Italy,
will be appointed Ambassador to France to succeed Dr. Sato
and that Mr. Shigenori Togo, Director of the European and
Asiatic Affairs Bureau of the Foreign Office, will take the
place of Ambassador Mushakoji in Berlin; that Mr. Masaaki
Hotta, Minister to Switzerland, will be named Ambassador
to Rome; and that Mr. Toshihiko Taketomi, Minister to the
Netherlands, will succeed Mr. Temasa Tokugawa as Ambassador
to Turkey. It was also reported that the Japanese Legation
in Polend would be raised to the status of an Embassy when

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 6, 1938.
Tokyo's 2938 of May 16, 1938, entitled
"Japan's China Policy and Proposed Reorganiza-
tion of the Japanese Foreign Office", gives an
illuminating account of the factors in Japan
responsible for the proposed drastic re-
organization of the Japanese Foreign Office,
whioh, it is understood, will include (1) con-
version of the present East Asia Bureau and
the Intelligence Section into quasi-independent
agenoies of the Government, although they would
remain under the direction of the Minister for
Foreign Affairs; (2) the redistribution of the
other business of the Foreign Office into four
geographical bureaus (Eastern European, Western
European, American, and South Seas), the Treaty
Bureau, and the Commercial Bureau; and (3) the
redesignation of the Ministry as the Ministry
of Foreign Policy. The Embassy observes that
the tide of nationalism which is being mani-
fested toward Government by directive organiza-
tion instead of by personal leadership has now
caught the Foreign Office and that there is
growing opposition to the conduct of foreign
relations by the small group of professional
diplomats and growing insistence that foreign
intercourse be based on that "immutable policy
toward China" which is now being implemented
by the armed services.
The Embassy further observes that there
has been a prevailing belief that the profes-
sional diplomats have not been disposed to make

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-8-
a positive contribution toward setting up a
foreign policy program unfettered by interna-
tional commitments calculated to preserve the
rights in the Far East of Occidental Powers.
This feeling bore fruit in the demand for the
setting up of an independent agency for the
centralization of control over all Japan's
interests in China, but the Foreign Office took
exception to the plan and the plan now put
forth is a compromise between the two conflict-
ing views. It is not known whether the plan
under discussion has been actually adopted by
the Foreign Minister and presented to the Prime
Minister, but it is a fact that its publication
was followed by criticism in influential
quarters, the substance of these criticisms
being that the entire nation has made sacrifioes
to implement Japan's continental policy and the
time has now come when exploitation of the
fruits of these sacrifices is a matter in which
all elements must share and not be left as a
special prerogative of any special group.
The Embassy further notes that for some
years there has been gradually taking place a
change in the type of men comprising the more
successful group of the Japanese Foreign Serv-
ice, the men entering the Service for the
past fifteen years being progressively more
parochial in their outlook and nationalistic
in their attitude. The Embassy concludes with
the observation that when the process of making
the Foreign Office an instrument of reactionary
forces

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
forces will have been completed it can be said
that the Foreign Office will then be more truly
refloctive of national aspirations than it has
been in recent years.
Comment: The contents of the despatch af-
ford very useful sidelight on the trend of af-
fairs in Japan. The developments discussed are
ominous of the difficulties which will face
Western Powers in the assertion of their rights
in the future in those parts of China under
Japanese control.

I have the honor to report that, according to the local
papers of May 9, a committee which has been studying the
problem of coordination within the Government of conduct
of foreign relations, has recommended a drastic reor-
FILED
ganization of the Foreign Office. As the recommenda-
2
tions of the committee have not as yet been made public,
G
3
3
a discussion of the changes proposed in detailed terms
would be premature, but it is understood that the present
East Asia Bureau and the Intelligence (Press) Section will
be
F/FC

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
be converted into quasi-independent agencies of the Govern-
ment, but placed under the direction of the Minister for
Foreign Affairs. The present business of the Foreign of-
fice, other than that now handled by the above-mentioned
offices, is to be redistributed between four geographical
bureaus (Eastern European, Western European, American and
South Seas) and the Treaty Bureau and the Commercial Bureau.
The Ministry is to be redesignated as the Ministry of Foreign
Policy.
The proposed changes do not reflect merely the desire
to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of the Foreign
Office. The tide of nationalism, which is being manifested
toward government by a directive organization instead of by
personal leadership, has now caught the Foreign Office.
There is growing opposition to the conduct of foreign re-
lations by a small group of professional di lomats, whose
training and experiences tend to develop an attitude of
liberalism, and growing insistence that foreign intercourse
be based on that "immutable policy toward China" which is
now being implemented by the armed services.
 Criticism of the "weak-kneed diplomacy" of the Japa-
nese Foreign Office has been the stock in trade of the re-
actionaries ever since the Foreign Office was instituted,
but such criticism has been especially vehement since the
outbreak of the Manchurian conflict in 1931. There has been
a prevailing belief for seme time that the professional
diplomats have not showm a disposition to make a positive
contribution toward setting up a foreign policy program
unfettered

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
unfettered by international commitments calculated largely
to preserve the rights in the Far East of Occidental powers.
This feeling bore fruit during the last session of the Diet
in a plan put forward by the Bureau of Legislation, one of
the offices placed under the Prime Minister, to set up
an independent agency of the Japanese Government where
there would be centralized control over all Japan's in-
terests in China, including the conduct of relations with
China and the development of economic interests in that
country. The Foreign Office took strong exception to this
plan, on the ground that relations with China needed to be
conducted with reference to Japan's relations with other
countries and in the light of conditions existing in other
parts of the world - that to isolate Chine relations would
lead to complete confusion. It seemed as if the matter
had there ended, especially when provision was made for the
establishment of two organizations for the economic develop-
ment of North Chine end Central China, respectively. How-
ever, whether the Foreign Office promised to make a counter
proposal to the Bureau of Legislation, or realized tlat danger
lay in lettin metters rest merely with rejecting the plan
of that Bureau, & comuittee of the Foreign Office began a
study of the organization of their Department cnd after two
months' vork brought forth the lan aoove-outlined.
We are informed by officials in the Foreign Ufrice that
this plan is a compromise. The senior officers in the
department - the Vice inister, r. Horinouchi, und the
bureau directors - tpparently fevor the mchins of modifications
in

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
in organization with a view to the elimination of friction
with other departments on matters relating to China, but
without structurally changing the department. The section
chiefs and the younger men, however, are agitating for
2
drastic changes - for the centralization of power in matters
relating to China, now distributed among the Finance, Over-
seas Affairs, and Commerce and Industry Ministries, along
with the Foreign Office.
We understand that the latter group first proposed
the setting up of a Board of East Asian Affairs, to embrace
the Manchuria Bureau of the Ministry of Overseas Affairs,
and the East Asia Bureau and the Intelligence Section of
the Foreign Office. This was to be an agency independent
of the Foreign Office, but to be under the direction of
the Minister for Foreign Affairs. The group of senior men
criticized the plan as unworkable, in which position the
Minister agreed, but it appears also that Mr. Hirota did
not think too well of the counterplan of the senior group,
which is, in short, an enlargement of the present East
Asia Bureau and the Press Section and their assimilation
into a Political Affairs Bureau of wich the director would
be the Vice Linister. The plan adopted by the Foreign Office
committee obviously contains certain features of both plans.
Whether the plan of reorganization has been formally
adopted by the Foreign Minister end will be presented to
the Prime Minister is not yet knowm. The important fact
is that publication of the plan was followed by a chorus
of derision from those yuarters whose views on public
matters now carry weight. The substance of their
observations

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.97)
812
- 5 -
observations was that the entire nation had made sacrifice
of "blood and treasure" to implement Japanese polioy on
the Continent, and that the time had come when the ex-
ploitation of the fruits of their sacrifices was a matter
in which all elements of the community must share, and not
be left as a special prerogative of the professional diplo-
matists or any other special group. The Prime Minister
has promised to look into the entire question of the adminis-
tration of Japanese interests in China, and one can expect
to see in due course some interesting developments which
will leave some substantial mark on the Foreign Office.
There has been taking place for some years a gradual
change in the type of men comprising the more successful
group of the Japanese Foreign Service. Such men as Baron
Hayashi, Viscount Ishii, and Mr. Matsudaira, who typify
the Japanese diplomatist of an epoch now past, are, by
their personality, character and enlightened ideals, quali-
11
fied to take a high place in any society. The standard
which formerly prevailed is being gradually and steadily
lowered by process of dilution, the men entering the ser-
vice for the past fifteen years being progressively more
parochial in their outlook and nationelistic in their
attitude. When the process of making the Foreign Office
an instrument of reactionary forces will have been com-
pleted, one will be able to make the conment that it

officials and official institutions responsible for Japanese
mancgement of affairs in China, the Legislative Bureau and the
Foreign Office prepared divergent plans. The Cabinet in
commenting on these two plans decided on March 15 to crente the
China Economic Affnirs Bureau to be placed under the direction
of the Cabinet. The Legislative Burenu had originally proposed
to establish a board under the direction of the Premier to be
entrusted with political, economical and cultural matters
relating to Chine and to be independent of the Foreign Office.
Foreign Minister Hirotn proposed to establish a bureau under
the direction of the Ministry for Foreign ffairs to handle only
the economic exploitation of Chine; political and cultural
matters were to be controlled as hitherto by the Foreign Office.
Under the plan referred to above as adopted by the Cabinet, there
will be cstablished a Chinc Economic Affairs Burenu which will
have no voice in diplomatic matters and the principal duty of
it will be to supervise the North Chinn Development Company and
the Central China Rohabilitation Company.

DEPARTMENT OFSTATE
Tokyo's despatch no. 4040, July 19,
1939, reperts on administrative changes in
the Japanese Foreign Office and Foreign
Service. Imperial Ordinance No. 465,
July 1l, 1939, enables the spec1al appoint-
ment of research officials from business and
professions for duty in the Foreign Office.
This reorganization of the researoh staff of
the Foreign Offioe is due in part to the
growing importance of Japan's foreign rela-
tions, and in part to the general demand for
administrative reform. The Embassy states that,
although the position of the new researoh of-
ficials has not been legally defined, it is
believed praotioally certain that they will
compose a new research bureau charged largely
with the study and determination of the larger
aspeots of Japanese foreign policy. The Embassy
states that army elements have for a long time
advocated establishment of a powerful policy-
determining body in the Foreign Office but were
opposed by the Privy Counoil; that the Privy
Council's opposition was oiroumvented by the
device of obtaining its consent to an increase
in officials while reserving the question of
organization to departmental regulation; and
that, while under the ordinance under reference
only

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2
enly six new offiolals will be appointed,
it is felt that a beginning has been made
teward drafting men from business and the
professions in contra-distinotion to the
bureauorat type usually found in Japanese
government offices.
Imperial Ordinance Ne. 466, July 1l,
1939, provides that Foreign Sertice officers
of Sonin rank may be appointed from among
persons capable of doing press information
work in Japanese embassies and consulates.
The Embassy states that these officials are
to do general propaganda work, and suggests
that they will be without the ethical guides
common to the diplomatio profession. The
Embassy suggests that an important problem
will be raised as to how such officials are
to be regarded, vested as they will be with
the status of diplomatio or consular representa

partioular importance to the United States
whose persistent and healthy tradition of

itself open to the widest abuses by non-
governmental foreign elements in the

of the new type will replace during the course of this
year those now at London, Faris, and New York, and new
posts will be created at Berlin, Rome, and Warsaw. Other
new posts are to be added us soon as circumstances permit.
The stationing of Japanese press attachés abroad who
by profession, training, and background are without as
 rule the ethical guides common to the diplomatic pro-
fession and whose work will consist, we may safely assume,
largely in the dissemination of propaganda rather than
the promotion of better relations and understanding raises,
particularly in those democratic countries who themselves
nave hitherto eschewed efforts at propaganda, the in-
teresting nd important problem of how such officials
are to be regarded, vested as they will be with the
status of diplomatic or consular representatives. The
problem would appear to be of particular importance to
the United St tes whose persistent nd healthy tr dition
of free sreech nd : free press normally lays itself open
to the widest buses by non-governmental foreign elements
in the population.
